=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a m x n 2D array board representing a chessboard, where board[i][j] represents the value of the cell (i, j).
Rooks in the same row or column attack each other. You need to place three rooks on the chessboard such that the rooks do not attack each other.
Return the maximum sum of the cell values on which the rooks are placed.
 
Example 1:

Input: board = [[-3,1,1,1],[-3,1,-3,1],[-3,2,1,1]]
Output: 4
Explanation:

We can place the rooks in the cells (0, 2), (1, 3), and (2, 1) for a sum of 1 + 1 + 2 = 4.

Example 2:

Input: board = [[1,2,3],[4,5,6],[7,8,9]]
Output: 15
Explanation:
We can place the rooks in the cells (0, 0), (1, 1), and (2, 2) for a sum of 1 + 5 + 9 = 15.

Example 3:

Input: board = [[1,1,1],[1,1,1],[1,1,1]]
Output: 3
Explanation:
We can place the rooks in the cells (0, 2), (1, 1), and (2, 0) for a sum of 1 + 1 + 1 = 3.

 
Constraints:

3 <= m == board.length <= 100
3 <= n == board[i].length <= 100
-10^9 <= board[i][j] <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maximumValueSum(self, board: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Implementation:

from typing import List
class Solution:
    def maximumValueSum(self, board: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        m = len(board)
        n = len(board[0])
        top = []
        for row in board:
            # get top 3 columns
            # Since n <=100, can sort:
            cols = sorted(range(n), key=lambda c: row[c], reverse=True)[:3]
            top.append([(row[c], c) for c in cols])
        ans = -10**30
        for i in range(m-2):
            for j in range(i+1, m-1):
                for k in range(j+1, m):
                    for v1,c1 in top[i]:
                        for v2,c2 in top[j]:
                            if c1 == c2:
                                continue
                            for v3,c3 in top[k]:
                                if c1 != c3 and c2 != c3:
                                    s = v1+v2+v3
                                    if s > ans:
                                        ans = s
        return ans

Let's ensure final code block includes no extra text. The user said "Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block." So final should be:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumValueSum(self, board: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        m = len(board)
        n = len(board[0])
        top = []
        for row in board:
            cols = sorted(range(n), key=lambda c: row[c], reverse=True)[:3]
            top.append([(row[c], c) for c in cols])

        ans = -10**30
        for i in range(m - 2):
            for j in range(i + 1, m - 1):
                for k in range(j + 1, m):
                    for v1, c1 in top[i]:
                        for v2, c2 in top[j]:
                            if c1 == c2:
                                continue
                            for v3, c3 in top[k]:
                                if c1 != c3 and c2 != c3:
                                    s = v1 + v2 + v3
                                    if s > ans:
                                        ans = s
        return ans
```

                    if c1 == c2: continue
                    for v3,c3 in tk:
                        if c1 != c3 and c2 != c3:

```
class Solution:
    def maximumValueSum(self, board: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        m, n = len(board), len(board[0])
        row = []
        for i in range(m):
            tmp = []
            for j in range(n):
                tmp.append((board[i][j], i, j))
            tmp.sort(reverse=True)
            row.append(tmp[:3])
        ans = -10**18
        for i in range(m):
            for j in range(i+1,m):
                for k in range(j+1,m):
                    for x in row[i]:
                        for y in row[j]:
                            for z in row[k]:
                                if x[2]!=y[2] and x[2]!=z[2] and y[2]!=z[2]:
                                    ans = max(ans, x[0]+y[0]+z[0])
        return ans
```
Yes.

Let's decide final code style. Maybe:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumValueSum(self, board: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        m, n = len(board), len(board[0])
        top = []
        for row in board:
            top.append(sorted(((val, col) for col, val in enumerate(row)), reverse=True)[:3])

        ans = -10**30
        for i in range(m - 2):
            for j in range(i + 1, m - 1):
                for k in range(j + 1, m):
                    for v1, c1 in top[i]:
                        for v2, c2 in top[j]:
                            if c1 == c2:
                                continue
                            for v3, c3 in top[k]:
                                if c1 != c3 and c2 != c3:
                                    ans = max(ans, v1 + v2 + v3)
        return ans
```

This is concise. But note `sorted(((val, col) for col, val in enumerate(row)), reverse=True)[:3]` sorts by val then col descending. If values equal, it chooses larger col. Arbitrary. Good.

Potential issue: If values are equal and columns descending, top3 may include columns 3,2,1 instead of 0,1,2. Still top3. Good.

Let's think about if there is any issue with `m, n = len(board), len(board[0])`; n unused. Could omit n. But okay. If n unused, no problem. Maybe use `_n`? Not needed. Could avoid lint? no.

Maybe use `m = len(board)` only. But `n` not needed. Use `m = len(board)`.

Let's write:

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumValueSum(self, board: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        m = len(board)
        top = []
        for row in board:
            top.append(sorted(((v, c) for c, v in enumerate(row)), reverse=True)[:3])

        ans = -10**30
        for i in range(m - 2):
            for j in range(i + 1, m - 1):
                for k in range(j + 1, m):
                    for v1, c1 in top[i]:
                        for v2, c2 in top[j]:
                            if c1 == c2:
                                continue
                            for v3, c3 in top[k]:
                                if c1 != c3 and c2 != c3:
                                    total = v1 + v2 + v3
                                    if total > ans:
                                        ans = total
        return ans
```

This is good.

Let's think about if there is any issue with `sorted(((v, c) for c, v in enumerate(row)), reverse=True)[:3]` if row is a list of ints; yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if values are equal and sorting by column descending means top3 columns maybe not the first 3 in original order; no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible issue with "maximum sum" if there are multiple optimal solutions and our top3 arbitrary but product includes one; yes.

Let's think about if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if a row has more than 3 columns with the same max value and top3 arbitrary but replacement with equal value could use a column that is not in top3? We choose from top3. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if there are negative values and sorted reverse=True on tuples `(v, c)` with v negative and c positive; no.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if there are values like -10^9 and -10^9, sorting stable? no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if `top` list contains tuples of length 2; inner loops unpack. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "maximumValueSum" if board is a list of lists but values are maybe numpy? no.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if there are only 3 rows and 4 columns, and row0 top3 columns 0,1,2; row1 uses col0, row2 uses col1; row0 can use col2. If row0 col2 value is lower than col3 but col3 is used by row1 or row2? Then col3 not available, but top3 by value would include col3 if greater, so top3 would be 0,1,3 maybe. Then row0 can use col2? Wait if col3 used by row1, top3 columns 0,1,3; row1 uses col0, row2 uses col1, row0 can use col3? But col3 used by row1, no. Let's construct: row0 values: c0=100,c1=99,c2=98,c3=150. Top3 c3,c0,c1. Other rows use c0,c1. Row0 can use c3=150, which is top3. Good. If other rows use c3 and c0, row0 can use c1=99. If row0 selected c2=98 not top3, replacement with c1=99. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if other rows use two of the top3 columns, leaving one. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible issue with "top3 per row" if there are only 3 rows and 4 columns, and row0 top3 columns 0,1,2; row1 uses col0, row2 uses col1; row0 can use col2. If row0 col2 value is lower than col3 but col3 is used by row1 or row2? Then col3 not available, but top3 by value would include col3 if greater, so top3 would be 0,1,3 maybe. Then row0 can use col2? Wait if col3 used by row1, top3 columns 0,1,3; row1 uses col0, row2 uses col1, row0 can use col3? But col3 used by row1, no.
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumValueSum(self, board: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        m = len(board)
        top = []

        for row in board:
            top.append(sorted(((v, c) for c, v in enumerate(row)), reverse=True)[:3])

        ans = -10**30

        for i in range(m - 2):
            for j in range(i + 1, m - 1):
                for k in range(j + 1, m):
                    for v1, c1 in top[i]:
                        for v2, c2 in top[j]:
                            if c1 == c2:
                                continue
                            for v3, c3 in top[k]:
                                if c1 != c3 and c2 != c3:
                                    total = v1 + v2 + v3
                                    if total > ans:
                                        ans = total

        return ans
```
